### 12.13-20 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-12-13-20"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

The devotee dear to the Lord: without hatred toward any being, friendly and compassionate, without 'mine' and ego, the same in pleasure and pain, forgiving; ever content, the yogin, self-controlled, firm in resolve, mind and understanding dedicated to the Lord; one by whom the world is not agitated and who is not agitated by the world, free from elation, anger, fear and anxiety; without expectation, pure, skilful, unconcerned, untroubled, renouncing all undertakings; neither rejoicing nor hating, neither grieving nor desiring, renouncing good and evil; the same to foe and friend, in honour and dishonour, cold and heat, pleasure and pain, free from attachment; the same in blame and praise, silent, content with whatever comes, without fixed home, steady of mind, full of devotion; and those who with faith, holding the Lord supreme, follow this nectar of dharma are exceedingly dear to him.

### 13.1-2 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-1-2"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

### 13.3-4 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-3-4"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Hear from me briefly what the field is, of what kind, with what modifications and whence, and who the knower is and what his powers; this has been sung by the seers in many ways, in distinct Vedic metres, and in the well-reasoned and decisive words of the Brahma-sūtra.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: teacher-transmission_

### 13.5-6 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-5-6"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: body-layers, consciousness-mind_

### 13.7-11 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-7-11"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

What is called knowledge: absence of pride and pretence, non-harm, patience, uprightness, service of the teacher, purity, steadiness, self-restraint; dispassion toward sense-objects, absence of ego, seeing the evil of birth, death, old age, disease and pain; non-attachment, not clinging to son, wife and home, constant evenness of mind in the desired and the undesired; unswerving devotion to me with undivided yoga, resorting to solitary places, distaste for the company of crowds; constancy in the knowledge of the self and insight into the goal of knowledge of the truth — this is declared to be knowledge; what is contrary is ignorance.

`sourced` · confidence high

> jñeyaṃ yat tat pravakṣyāmi yaj jñātvāmṛtam aśnute anādimat paraṃ brahma na sat tan nāsad ucyate

terms: [brahman](../terms/brahman.md)

### 13.13-17 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-13-17"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

With hands and feet everywhere, eyes, heads and faces everywhere, with ears everywhere, it stands enveloping everything in the world; shining through the functions of all the senses yet free of all senses, unattached yet supporting all, free of the guṇas yet experiencing the guṇas; outside and inside beings, unmoving and moving, too subtle to be known, far and yet near; undivided yet standing as if divided among beings, the supporter of beings, their devourer and creator; the light of lights, said to be beyond darkness, knowledge, the knowable and the goal of knowledge, seated in the heart of all.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: apophatic · path: knowledge · stage: advanced · types: ultimate_

terms: [brahman](../terms/brahman.md) · concepts: [The Lord in the heart of all beings](../concepts/lord-in-the-heart.md)

`sourced` · confidence high

### 13.19-20 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-19-20"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Know that prakṛti and puruṣa are both beginningless, and that the modifications and the guṇas arise from prakṛti; prakṛti is said to be the cause in the production of effect, instrument and agency, and puruṣa the cause in the experiencing of pleasure and pain.

> prakṛtiṃ puruṣaṃ caiva viddhy anādī ubhāv api vikārāṃś ca guṇāṃś caiva viddhi prakṛtisaṃbhavān kāryakāraṇakartṛtve hetuḥ prakṛtir ucyate puruṣaḥ sukhaduḥkhānāṃ bhoktṛtve hetur ucyate

_level: ultimate · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: ultimate_

### 13.19-23 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-13-19-23"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Know that prakṛti and puruṣa are both beginningless, and that the modifications and guṇas are born of prakṛti; prakṛti is called the cause in the producing of effect, instrument and agency, puruṣa the cause in the experiencing of pleasure and pain; puruṣa, seated in prakṛti, experiences the guṇas born of prakṛti, and attachment to the guṇas is the cause of his births in good and evil wombs; the supreme Puruṣa in this body is called the witness, the permitter, the supporter, the experiencer, the great Lord and the supreme self; one who thus knows puruṣa and prakṛti with its guṇas is not born again, however he may live.

_level: ultimate · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: ultimate, karma-liberation_

### 14.6-8 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-14-6-8"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Sattva, being stainless, illuminating and free of disease, binds by attachment to happiness and to knowledge; rajas, whose nature is passion, arising from thirst and attachment, binds the embodied one by attachment to action; tamas, born of ignorance and deluding all embodied beings, binds by negligence, sloth and sleep.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: consciousness-mind, karma-liberation_

`sourced` · confidence high

Sattva attaches to happiness, rajas to action, while tamas, veiling knowledge, attaches to negligence; sattva prevails by overpowering rajas and tamas, rajas by overpowering sattva and tamas, and tamas by overpowering sattva and rajas.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: consciousness-mind_

`sourced` · confidence high

`sourced` · confidence high

`sourced` · confidence high

He does not hate illumination, activity and delusion when they arise, nor long for them when they cease; seated as one indifferent, he is not disturbed by the guṇas and, knowing that it is the guṇas that act, stands firm and does not waver; the same in pleasure and pain, abiding in the self, regarding a clod, a stone and gold as equal, the same toward the pleasant and the unpleasant, steady, the same in blame and praise; the same in honour and dishonour, to friend and foe, renouncing all undertakings — he is said to have gone beyond the guṇas.

### 16.9-18 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-16-9-18"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Holding this view, these lost souls of small understanding and cruel deeds rise up as enemies of the world for its destruction; given to insatiable desire, full of hypocrisy, pride and arrogance, they act with impure resolves; beset by innumerable cares ending only with death, they think: 'this I have gained today, this desire I shall fulfil; this enemy I have slain and others I shall slay; I am the lord, I am the enjoyer, I am successful, powerful and happy'; self-conceited, they sacrifice in name only, with ostentation and not according to rule, and hate me in their own bodies and in others'.

### 17.7-10 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-17-7-10"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Food dear to all is threefold, as are sacrifice, austerity and giving: foods that increase life, vitality, strength, health, happiness and cheerfulness, that are savoury, oily, substantial and agreeable, are dear to the sāttvic; foods that are bitter, sour, salty, very hot, pungent, dry and burning, producing pain, grief and disease, are desired by the rājasic; food that is stale, tasteless, putrid, left over from the previous day, remnants and impure, is dear to the tāmasic.

`sourced` · confidence high

_level: conventional · standpoint: seeker · path: general · stage: all · types: practice, ethics_

### 17.17-19 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-17-17-19"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

This threefold austerity practised with supreme faith by yoked people who desire no fruit is sāttvic; austerity practised for respect, honour and reverence, with hypocrisy, is rājasic, unstable and fleeting; austerity practised with a foolish obstinacy, by torturing oneself, or to destroy another, is tāmasic.

`sourced` · confidence high

Therefore the acts of sacrifice, giving and austerity enjoined by scripture are always begun by the expounders of Brahman with the utterance of 'Oṃ'; with 'tat', without aiming at the fruit, the seekers of liberation perform acts of sacrifice, austerity and giving; 'sat' is used in the sense of reality and of goodness, and for a praiseworthy act; steadfastness in sacrifice, austerity and giving is also called 'sat', as is any action for that purpose.

### 18.20-22 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-20-22"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Knowledge by which one sees one imperishable being in all beings, undivided in the divided, is sāttvic; knowledge that sees in all beings manifold separate entities of different kinds is rājasic; knowledge that clings to one effect as if it were the whole, without reason, without grasp of truth and trifling, is tāmasic.

> sarvabhūteṣu yenaikaṃ bhāvam avyayam īkṣate avibhaktaṃ vibhakteṣu taj jñānaṃ viddhi sāttvikam pṛthaktvena tu yaj jñānaṃ nānābhāvān pṛthagvidhān vetti sarveṣu bhūteṣu taj jñānaṃ viddhi rājasam yat tu kṛtsnavad ekasmin kārye saktam ahaitukam atattvārthavad alpaṃ ca tat tāmasam udāhṛtam

### 18.23-25 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-23-25"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

### 18.26-28 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-26-28"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

An agent free from attachment, without egoism, endowed with steadiness and zeal, unchanged in success and failure, is sāttvic; one who is passionate, desires the fruit of action, greedy, harmful, impure and moved by joy and sorrow is rājasic; one who is unyoked, vulgar, obstinate, deceitful, malicious, lazy, despondent and procrastinating is tāmasic.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: action · stage: all · types: ethics_

concepts: [The guṇa-classification of knowledge, action, agent, intellect, steadfastness and happiness](../concepts/guna-typology-of-gita-18.md)

### 18.29-32 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-29-32"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

The understanding that knows the way of action and of withdrawal, what is to be done and not done, what is to be feared and not feared, bondage and liberation, is sāttvic; the understanding that knows dharma and adharma, what is to be done and not, wrongly, is rājasic; the understanding that, enveloped in darkness, takes adharma for dharma and sees all things perversely, is tāmasic.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: knowledge · stage: all · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [buddhi](../terms/buddhi.md) · concepts: [The guṇa-classification of knowledge, action, agent, intellect, steadfastness and happiness](../concepts/guna-typology-of-gita-18.md)

### 18.33-35 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-33-35"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

The steadfastness (dhṛti) by which, through unswerving yoga, one holds the activities of mind, breath and senses is sāttvic; that by which one holds to dharma, pleasure and wealth with attachment, desiring the fruit, is rājasic; that by which a fool does not let go of sleep, fear, grief, dejection and conceit is tāmasic.

_level: conventional · standpoint: analytic · path: meditation, general · stage: all · types: consciousness-mind_

terms: [dhṛti](../terms/dhrti.md) · concepts: [The guṇa-classification of knowledge, action, agent, intellect, steadfastness and happiness](../concepts/guna-typology-of-gita-18.md)

### 18.36-39 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-18-36-39"></a>
`sourced` · confidence high

Happiness in which one comes to delight through practice and reaches the end of sorrow — which is like poison at first but like nectar in the end, born of the serenity of one's own understanding — is sāttvic; happiness from the contact of the senses with their objects, like nectar at first but like poison in the end, is rājasic; happiness that deludes the self at the beginning and at the end, arising from sleep, sloth and negligence, is tāmasic.

`sourced` · confidence high

### ch1 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch1"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 1 sets the scene and the crisis. 1.1: Dhṛtarāṣṭra asks Sañjaya what the two assembled armies did at Kurukṣetra, 'the field of dharma'. 1.2–11: Duryodhana goes to his teacher Droṇa, lists the leading warriors of the Pāṇḍava army (1.4–6) and of his own (1.8–9), compares the two forces (1.10) and orders that Bhīṣma be guarded (1.11). 1.12–19: Bhīṣma and then the Pāṇḍava side (Kṛṣṇa, Arjuna, Bhīma, Yudhiṣṭhira, Nakula, Sahadeva and others) blow their named conches; the uproar rends the Kauravas' hearts. 1.20–27: Arjuna asks Kṛṣṇa to station the chariot between the armies; Kṛṣṇa does so and tells him to look at the assembled Kurus; Arjuna sees his elders, teachers and kin on both sides. 1.28–46: overcome by pity (kṛpā) and despair (viṣāda), Arjuna describes his bodily collapse (1.28–30), says he sees no good (śreyas) in killing kin and wants neither victory nor kingship (1.31–37), argues that destroying a family destroys the family dharmas, leads to varṇa-mixture, the fall of the ancestors and hell (1.38–44), and says it would be better to be killed unresisting (1.45–46). 1.47: Arjuna sits down in the chariot, casting away his bow, his mind overwhelmed by grief (śoka). The chapter's purpose, as it presents itself, is to state Arjuna's grief and his argument from family dharma, which the following teaching answers.

_level: conventional · standpoint: ethical-social · path: general · stage: unmarked · types: narrative, ethics_

terms: [dharma](../terms/dharma.md), [kuladharma](../terms/kuladharma.md), [varṇasaṅkara](../terms/varnasankara.md), [śoka](../terms/soka.md), [viṣāda](../terms/visada.md), [kṛpā](../terms/krpa.md) · concepts: [Family dharma (kula-dharma)](../concepts/kuladharma.md), [Mixture of the varṇas (varṇa-saṅkara)](../concepts/varnasankara.md) · teachers: [Dhṛtarāṣṭra](../teachers/dhrtarastra.md), [Sañjaya](../teachers/sanjaya.md), [Duryodhana](../teachers/duryodhana.md), [Droṇa](../teachers/drona.md), [Bhīṣma (Devavrata, Gāṅgeya)](../teachers/bhisma.md), [Kṛṣṇa (Vāsudeva)](../teachers/krsna.md), [Arjuna (Pārtha, Dhanaṃjaya)](../teachers/arjuna.md) · disputes: [Should Arjuna fight the battle in which his elders, teachers and kinsmen stand on the other side?](../debates/bhagavad-gita-arjuna-should-fight.md)

### ch2 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch2"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 2 opens the teaching. 2.1–10 (frame): Kṛṣṇa rebukes Arjuna's faintness and unmanliness (2.2–3); Arjuna restates his dilemma — he cannot fight Bhīṣma and Droṇa, would rather live on alms, does not know which outcome is better — and asks Kṛṣṇa, as his disciple who has come for refuge, to tell him for certain what is better (śreyas) (2.4–8); he says 'I will not fight' and falls silent, and Kṛṣṇa, as if smiling, answers (2.9–10). 2.11–30: the teaching on the embodied self (dehin): the wise do not grieve; the self was never not and will never cease; it passes through bodies as through the ages of life and as through garments; what is not (asat) has no being and what is (sat) no non-being; the self neither slays nor is slain, is unborn, eternal, uncuttable, unmanifest and unthinkable; even on the contrary view birth and death are inevitable; therefore do not grieve. 2.31–38: the argument from one's own dharma (svadharma) and honour: a dharmic battle is the kṣatriya's best good and a door to heaven, refusal brings sin and disgrace; fight treating pleasure and pain, gain and loss, victory and defeat alike. 2.39: transition — the understanding (buddhi) given 'in sāṃkhya' is now given 'in yoga'. 2.40–53: the yoga of understanding (buddhi-yoga): resolute understanding is one (2.41); the flowery speech of those who delight in Vedic statements and seek heaven and power keeps understanding from samādhi; the Vedas concern the three guṇas, be free of them (2.42–46); your right is to action, not its fruits; yoga is sameness and skill in actions; the wise, yoked with understanding, abandon the fruit and are freed from the bondage of birth (2.47–51); when understanding passes beyond delusion and stands steady in samādhi, you will attain yoga (2.52–53). 2.54–72: Arjuna asks about the person of steady wisdom (sthitaprajña); Kṛṣṇa describes him: he gives up all desires and is content in the self, unshaken by sorrow and pleasure, free from passion, fear and anger, withdraws the senses like a tortoise (2.55–61); the chain from dwelling on objects through attachment, desire, anger, delusion and loss of memory to ruin (2.62–63), and its reverse through mastered senses to serenity and steady understanding (2.64–68); the sage is awake where beings sleep, is filled by desires like the ocean yet unmoved, and without 'mine' and 'I' attains peace (2.69–71); this is the brahmic state, reaching which even at death one attains brahma-nirvāṇa (2.72). The chapter's purpose, as it presents itself, is to answer Arjuna's grief and his request for the good, first by knowledge of the self, then by the discipline of action done with steady understanding, culminating in the portrait of the one whose wisdom is steady.

terms: [dehin](../terms/dehin.md), [svadharma](../terms/svadharma.md), [sāṃkhya](../terms/samkhya.md), [yoga](../terms/yoga.md), [buddhiyoga](../terms/buddhi-yoga.md), [sthitaprajña](../terms/sthitaprajna.md), [brahmanirvāṇa](../terms/brahma-nirvana.md), [samatva](../terms/samatva.md) · concepts: [The eternal, unslayable embodied one (dehin / śarīrin)](../concepts/imperishable-embodied-self.md), [Action without attachment to its fruit](../concepts/niskama-karma.md), [The person of steady wisdom (sthitaprajña)](../concepts/sthitaprajna.md), [One's own dharma (svadharma)](../concepts/svadharma.md), [From dwelling on objects to ruin (BhG 2.62–63)](../concepts/descent-from-sense-objects-to-ruin.md) · teachers: [Kṛṣṇa (Vāsudeva)](../teachers/krsna.md), [Arjuna (Pārtha, Dhanaṃjaya)](../teachers/arjuna.md), [Sañjaya](../teachers/sanjaya.md) · disputes: [Should Arjuna fight the battle in which his elders, teachers and kinsmen stand on the other side?](../debates/bhagavad-gita-arjuna-should-fight.md), [Are the Vedic rites that hold out heaven, enjoyment and power all there is to seek ('there is nothing else')?](../debates/bhagavad-gita-vedavada.md)

### ch3 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch3"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 3 answers the question of understanding versus action. 3.1–2: Arjuna asks why, if understanding (buddhi) is held superior to action, Kṛṣṇa urges him to terrible action, and asks for one decisive teaching. 3.3–9: Kṛṣṇa says he declared of old a twofold commitment (niṣṭhā) — the yoga of knowledge for the sāṃkhyas and the yoga of action for the yogins; actionlessness (naiṣkarmya) is not reached by not acting nor perfection by mere renunciation; no one can stay without acting, since the guṇas of prakṛti make everyone act; restraining the organs of action while the mind dwells on objects is hypocrisy, while karma-yoga with the senses controlled and without attachment excels; do your allotted action, for the world is bound by action except action for sacrifice. 3.10–16: Prajāpati created beings with sacrifice, telling them to nourish the gods and be nourished; who enjoys the gods' gifts without offering is a thief; eating the remains of sacrifice frees from fault; the wheel of food, rain, sacrifice, action, brahman and the imperishable must be kept turning. 3.17–18: for one who delights and is content in the self there is nothing that must be done. 3.19–26: therefore act unattached; Janaka and others reached perfection by action; act for the holding together of the world (loka-saṃgraha), since people follow the best; Kṛṣṇa himself, needing nothing, keeps acting lest the worlds perish; the wise should act unattached as the unwise act attached, and not unsettle the understanding of the ignorant. 3.27–29: actions are done by the guṇas, and only one deluded by the sense of 'I' (ahaṃkāra) thinks 'I am the doer'; the knower of the truth is not attached. 3.30–32: resign all actions to Kṛṣṇa and fight; those who follow this teaching with faith are released, those who cavil are lost. 3.33–35: all beings follow their nature; attraction and aversion lie in wait in the sense-objects; one's own dharma, though imperfect, is better than another's. 3.36–43: Arjuna asks what drives a person to sin against his will; Kṛṣṇa names desire and anger, born of rajas, the enemy that covers knowledge as smoke covers fire, seated in the senses, mind and understanding; restrain the senses first, know what is beyond understanding, steady the self by the self and slay desire. The chapter's purpose, as it presents itself, is to show why Arjuna must act — action being unavoidable, sacrificial, exemplary and non-binding when done without attachment — and to name desire as what must be overcome.

(BhG 3.1–8, 5.1–6, 6.1–4, 18.1–12)](../debates/renunciation-or-action-gita.md), `dsp:works-knowledge-grace`

### ch4 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch4"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 4 moves in six steps. (1) 4.1–3: the Lord declares that he taught this imperishable yoga to Vivasvat, who told it to Manu, and Manu to Ikṣvāku; known by the royal sages through succession, it was lost over a long time, and he now teaches the same ancient yoga to Arjuna because he is his devotee and friend — it is the highest secret. (2) 4.4–10: to Arjuna's question how he could have taught it to Vivasvat, who was born earlier, the Lord answers that he knows his many births and Arjuna's; though unborn and lord of beings, he comes into being by his own māyā whenever dharma declines, to protect the good, destroy wrongdoers and establish dharma, age after age; whoever knows his divine birth and action is not reborn but comes to him, as many have, purified by the austerity of knowledge. (3) 4.11–15: he receives people in the way they approach him; those who want quick success of their actions sacrifice to the deities; he made the fourfold class order by the division of guṇa and action, yet is a non-doer, unstained by action; knowing this the ancient seekers of liberation acted, so Arjuna should act. (4) 4.16–23: what action, wrong action (vikarma) and inaction are; the wise one sees inaction in action and action in inaction; free of desire and intention, his actions burnt in the fire of knowledge, unattached to the fruit, he is not bound though acting, and for one who acts for the sake of sacrifice action dissolves. (5) 4.24–32: action as sacrifice — every element of the offering is Brahman — and a list of the many kinds of sacrifice (to the gods; sacrifice offered into the fire of Brahman; the senses into the fires of restraint; the sense-objects into the fires of the senses; the actions of the senses and of breath into the fire of self-restraint; sacrifice of material things, austerity, yoga, recitation and knowledge; prāṇa and apāna offered into each other; breaths into breaths by those regulated in food), all born of action and all destroying impurity. (6) 4.33–42: the sacrifice of knowledge is better, since all action culminates in knowledge; knowledge is to be learned from seers of the truth by prostration, questioning and service; it is a raft over all wrongdoing, a fire that burns all actions, the supreme purifier, gained by the faithful and self-restrained and bringing supreme peace, while the ignorant, faithless doubter perishes; actions do not bind one who has renounced action by yoga and cut doubt by knowledge; the chapter ends: cut this doubt with the sword of knowledge, resort to yoga and stand up. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to ground the teaching in an ancient transmission and in the Lord who gives it, and to show that action done as sacrifice, without attachment and with knowledge, does not bind and culminates in knowledge, so that Arjuna may act free of doubt.

terms: [yoga](../terms/yoga.md), [karma](../terms/karma.md), [akarman](../terms/akarma.md), [vikarma](../terms/vikarma.md), [yajña](../terms/yajna.md), [jñāna-yajña](../terms/jnana-yajna.md), [jñānāgni](../terms/jnanagni.md) · concepts: [Teacher, transmission and secrecy in the Gītā and epic](../concepts/gita-transmission.md), [The Lord's descent (avatāra / prādurbhāva)](../concepts/avatara.md), [Action, inaction and wrong action](../concepts/karma-akarma-vikarma.md), [Sacrifice reinterpreted (yajña)](../concepts/yajna-reinterpreted.md), [The paths of the Gītā: action, knowledge, meditation and devotion](../concepts/four-yogas-of-the-gita.md) · teachers: [Kṛṣṇa (Vāsudeva)](../teachers/krsna.md), [Arjuna (Pārtha, Dhanaṃjaya)](../teachers/arjuna.md), [Vivasvat (the Sun)](../teachers/vivasvat.md), [Manu Vaivasvata](../teachers/manu-vaivasvata.md), [Ikṣvāku](../teachers/iksvaku.md)

### ch5 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch5"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 5 moves in seven steps. (1) 5.1: Arjuna asks which is better, the renunciation of actions (saṃnyāsa) or yoga, since Kṛṣṇa praises both. (2) 5.2–6: both lead to the highest good, but the yoga of action is superior to the renunciation of action; the constant renouncer is the one who neither hates nor desires; only the childish, not the learned, call Sāṃkhya and Yoga separate, since the same place is reached by both; renunciation is hard to attain without yoga, while the sage yoked in yoga soon reaches Brahman. (3) 5.7–12: the one yoked in yoga, whose self has become the self of all beings, is not stained though acting; the yoked knower of reality thinks 'I do nothing', holding that the senses move among their objects; one who acts placing actions in Brahman and abandoning attachment is unstained, like a lotus leaf by water; yogins act for the purification of the self; the yoked one who gives up the fruit attains lasting peace, while the unyoked, attached to the fruit through desire, is bound. (4) 5.13–17: the embodied one, having renounced all actions with the mind, dwells at ease in the city of nine gates, neither acting nor causing action; the master (prabhu) creates neither agency nor actions nor their link with the fruit — inherent nature (svabhāva) acts; knowledge is covered by ignorance and so creatures are deluded, but where ignorance is destroyed by knowledge, knowledge illumines the highest like the sun, and those intent on That go to non-return. (5) 5.18–26: the learned see the same in all beings; those whose mind rests in sameness conquer creation here and are established in Brahman, which is flawless and the same; the knower of Brahman is unmoved by the pleasant and the unpleasant; unattached to external contacts he finds the happiness that is in the self, for contact-born enjoyments are wombs of sorrow; one who withstands the surge of desire and anger is yoked and happy; the yogin with happiness, delight and light within, having become Brahman, attains brahma-nirvāṇa, which lies near the self-knowing ascetics free of desire and anger. (6) 5.27–28: in outline, a practice (external contacts put outside, the gaze between the brows, prāṇa and apāna made even) of the sage intent on liberation, who is ever free. (7) 5.29: knowing the Lord as enjoyer of sacrifices and austerities, great lord of all the worlds and friend of all beings, one attains peace. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to answer the question of renunciation versus the yoga of action by showing that true renunciation is inward, that the two reach the same goal, and that the goal is brahma-nirvāṇa and peace.

_level: unmarked · standpoint: seeker · path: action, knowledge, meditation · stage: all · types: practice, karma-liberation, dispute, ultimate_

(BhG 3.1–8, 5.1–6, 6.1–4, 18.1–12)](../debates/renunciation-or-action-gita.md)

### ch6 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch6"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 6 moves in nine steps. (1) 6.1–4: the one who does the action that is to be done without depending on its fruit is both renouncer and yogin, not one who merely gives up the sacred fires and rites; what is called renunciation is yoga, for no one becomes a yogin without renouncing intention (saṅkalpa); for the sage wishing to ascend to yoga action is the means, for the one who has ascended calm (śama) is the means, and he has ascended when he is attached neither to sense-objects nor to actions. (2) 6.5–9: one should raise the self by the self, which is its own friend or enemy; for the self-conquered and tranquil, amid the pairs of opposites, the supreme self (paramātman) is established — or, on the other construal, his self is supremely composed; the yoked yogin, satisfied with knowledge and realized knowledge, sees clod, stone and gold alike and is even in intellect (sama-buddhi) toward friend and foe, the good and the wicked. (3) 6.10–17: instruction in meditation — solitude, a firm seat in a clean place, the mind (manas) made one-pointed, body, head and neck held in line, the gaze toward the nose-tip, fearlessness, the vow of the brahmacārin, the mind (manas) restrained, thought (citta) on the Lord and intent on him — leading to the peace that culminates in nirvāṇa; yoga is for one measured in food, recreation, effort, sleep and waking. (4) 6.18–23: the state of yoga: restrained thought (citta) resting in the self alone, steady like a lamp in a windless place; thought (citta) stilled (niruddha), the self seen by the self, boundless happiness beyond the senses, no gain counted higher, not shaken even by heavy sorrow — this is to be known as yoga, the unyoking from union with sorrow, to be practised with resolve and an undejected mind (cetas). (5) 6.24–28: the method: abandoning desires born of intention and restraining the senses, coming to rest little by little with an intellect held firm, making the mind abide in the self and thinking of nothing, bringing the restless mind back whenever it wanders; the result: supreme happiness, becoming Brahman, the boundless happiness of contact with Brahman. (6) 6.29–32: the yoked one sees the self in all beings and all beings in the self; whoever sees the Lord everywhere and everything in him is not lost to him; the yogin established in oneness who worships him as abiding in all beings abides in him however he lives; the highest yogin sees pleasure and pain everywhere by likeness to himself. (7) 6.33–36: Arjuna objects that the mind is too restless for this yoga of sameness to stand steady; the Lord grants that it is hard to restrain but says it is held by practice (abhyāsa) and dispassion (vairāgya), and that yoga is attainable by the self-controlled who strive by the right means. (8) 6.37–45: Arjuna asks the fate of the one with faith who does not strive and falls from yoga; the Lord answers that no doer of good comes to a bad end: he dwells in the worlds of the meritorious, is reborn among the pure and prosperous or, more rarely, among wise yogins, regains the understanding of his former body, is carried on by former practice, and, perfected over many births, reaches the highest goal. (9) 6.46–47: the yogin is greater than ascetics, knowers and performers of works, so Arjuna should be a yogin; and of all yogins the one who worships the Lord with faith, his inner self gone into him, is the most yoked. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to set out the discipline of meditative yoga — its prerequisites, method, state and result — to reassure the practitioner about restlessness and failure, and to place faithful worship of the Lord at the summit of yoga.

### ch7 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch7"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 7 moves in eight steps. (1) 7.1–3: with his mind attached to the Lord, practising yoga and taking refuge in him, Arjuna will know him entirely (samagram) and without doubt; the Lord will declare knowledge together with vijñāna, after which nothing further remains to be known; among thousands scarcely one strives for perfection, and of those who strive scarcely one knows him in truth. (2) 7.4–7: his two natures — the lower, divided eightfold (earth, water, fire, air, space, mind, intellect and the sense of 'I'), and the higher, which has become the living being and by which the world is upheld; all beings have these two as their womb; he is the origin and the dissolution of the whole world; nothing is higher than him, and all this is strung on him like gems on a thread. (3) 7.8–12: he is the essence in things — the taste in water, the light of moon and sun, Oṃ in all the Vedas, sound in space, manhood in men, fragrance in earth, brilliance in fire, life in all beings, austerity in ascetics, the eternal seed of beings, the intelligence of the intelligent, the strength of the strong free of desire and passion, desire not opposed to dharma; the states of sattva, rajas and tamas come from him, yet he is not in them — they are in him. (4) 7.13–15: deluded by these guṇa-made states the world does not know him; his divine māyā made of the guṇas is hard to cross, but those who take refuge in him alone cross it; evil-doers of demonic disposition, their knowledge carried off by māyā, do not take refuge in him. (5) 7.16–19: four kinds of people of good deeds worship him — the distressed, the one who wishes to know, the seeker of gain and the knower; all are noble, but the knower, ever yoked and of single devotion, is foremost, mutually dear with him and held by him to be his very self; at the end of many births the knower takes refuge in him, knowing 'Vāsudeva is all' — a great soul very hard to find. (6) 7.20–23: those whose knowledge desires have carried off resort to other deities, following their observances; whatever form a devotee wishes to worship with faith, he makes that faith firm, and through it the worshipper obtains desires ordained by him; but the fruit is finite — worshippers of the gods go to the gods, his devotees come to him. (7) 7.24–26: the unintelligent think of him as unmanifest come into manifestation, not knowing his higher, imperishable state; veiled by his yoga-māyā he is not manifest to all; he knows all beings past, present and to come, but no one knows him. (8) 7.27–30: all beings fall into bewilderment at birth through the delusion of the pairs born of desire and aversion; those of meritorious deeds whose sin has ended worship him firm in their vows; those who take refuge in him and strive for release from old age and death know Brahman, the adhyātma and action completely, and those who know him with the adhibhūta, the adhidaiva and the adhiyajña know him even at the time of departure — terms that Arjuna asks about in 8.1–2. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to give the knowledge of the Lord 'entire' (samagra), together with vijñāna, and to show refuge in him as the way across his māyā.

### ch8 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch8"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 8 moves in nine steps. (1) 8.1–2: Arjuna asks seven questions — what is that Brahman, what is the adhyātma, what is action, what are the adhibhūta and the adhidaiva, who and how is the adhiyajña here in the body, and how is the Lord to be known at the time of departure by the self-controlled. (2) 8.3–4: the Lord's answers: Brahman is the supreme imperishable; own being (svabhāva) is called the adhyātma; the sending-forth (visarga) that brings about beings' states of existence is called action; the adhibhūta is the perishable state, the adhidaivata puruṣa ('person'), and the adhiyajña the Lord himself, here in the body. (3) 8.5–8: whoever departs at the end remembering him alone comes to his state; one goes to whatever state one remembers on leaving the body, having always been shaped by it; therefore Arjuna is to remember him at all times and fight, with mind and intellect offered to him; with thought (cetas) yoked by the yoga of repeated practice one goes to the supreme divine Person. (4) 8.9–13: the departure of one who remembers: whoever at departure, with unmoving mind, devotion and the power of yoga, the breath made to enter between the brows, remembers the ancient seer, subtler than the subtle, sun-coloured, beyond darkness, reaches that supreme divine Person; the imperishable that knowers of the Veda speak of and ascetics enter is declared in brief: whoever departs having restrained the gates, confined the mind in the heart and placed the breath in the head, uttering Oṃ, the one-syllabled Brahman, and remembering him, goes to the supreme goal. (5) 8.14–16: for the ever-yoked yogin who remembers him constantly with thought on nothing else (ananya-cetas) he is easy to reach; the great souls who come to him are not reborn into the impermanent abode of sorrow, whereas all worlds up to Brahmā's are subject to return. (6) 8.17–19: Brahmā's day lasts a thousand yugas and his night as long; at the coming of day manifest things come forth from the unmanifest, at the coming of night they dissolve into it, and the same multitude of beings comes to be again and again, helplessly. (7) 8.20–22: beyond that unmanifest is another, eternal unmanifest being that does not perish when all beings perish; it is called the imperishable and the supreme goal, from which they do not return — his supreme abode; that supreme Person, in whom beings abide and by whom all is pervaded, is gained by undivided devotion. (8) 8.23–27: the times at which departing yogins go to non-return or to return: fire, light, day, the bright fortnight and the six months of the northern course lead the knowers of Brahman to Brahman; smoke, night, the dark fortnight and the six months of the southern course lead the yogin to the lunar light and return; these two courses are everlasting for the world, and knowing them no yogin is deluded — so Arjuna is to be yoked in yoga at all times. (9) 8.28: having known this, the yogin goes beyond all the merit declared for the Vedas, sacrifices, austerities and gifts and reaches the supreme, primal place. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to answer the questions left open at 7.29–30, above all how the Lord is known at death, and to show that constant remembrance of him, carried through to the moment of departure, leads to the imperishable from which there is no return.

_level: unmarked · standpoint: seeker · path: knowledge, meditation, devotion · stage: all · types: ultimate, death-dying, world-fate, practice_

terms: [akṣara](../terms/aksara.md), [adhyātma](../terms/adhyatma.md), [adhibhūta](../terms/adhibhuta.md), [adhidaiva](../terms/adhidaiva.md), [adhiyajña](../terms/adhiyajna.md), [antakāla](../terms/antakala.md), [oṃ](../terms/om.md), [avyakta](../terms/avyakta.md), [brahman](../terms/brahman.md), [adhidaivata](../terms/adhidaivata.md), [uttarāyaṇa](../terms/uttarayana.md), [anāvṛtti](../terms/anavrtti.md) · concepts: [The three planes: divine, bodily, sacrificial](../concepts/adhidaiva-adhyatma-adhiyajna.md), [The last thought at death](../concepts/last-thought-at-death.md), [The day and night of Brahmā](../concepts/day-and-night-of-brahma.md), [The two paths after death (bright and dark)](../concepts/two-paths-after-death.md), [The imperishable (akṣara)](../concepts/aksara.md), [Yogic departure at death](../concepts/yogic-exit-at-death.md), [The manifest and the unmanifest](../concepts/vyakta-and-avyakta.md), [Rebirth](../concepts/rebirth.md), [Liberation (mokṣa) in later Mīmāṃsā](../concepts/liberation.md) · practices: [Remembering the Lord at the hour of death](../practices/antakala-smarana.md), [Constant remembrance of the Lord (smaraṇa)](../practices/smarana.md), [Yogic departure at death](../practices/utkranti-yoga.md) · teachers: [Kṛṣṇa (Vāsudeva)](../teachers/krsna.md), [Arjuna (Pārtha, Dhanaṃjaya)](../teachers/arjuna.md)

### ch9 <a id="tea-bhagavad-gita-ch9"></a>
`text-verified` · confidence high

Chapter 9 moves in nine steps. (1) 9.1–3: to Arjuna, who does not cavil, the Lord will declare the most secret knowledge together with vijñāna, knowing which he will be freed from what is inauspicious — the royal knowledge, the royal secret, the supreme purifier, directly apprehended, in accord with dharma, very easy to practise, imperishable; those without faith in this dharma do not reach him and return to the path of death and saṃsāra. (2) 9.4–10: all this is pervaded by him in unmanifest form; all beings abide in him, but he is not established in them — and yet beings do not abide in him: 'behold my lordly yoga'; his self sustains beings and brings them into being without abiding in them, as the great wind always abides in space; at the close of a kalpa beings go into his prakṛti and at its beginning he sends them forth again, taking hold of his own prakṛti; these acts do not bind him, seated as one indifferent and unattached; with him as overseer prakṛti gives birth to the moving and the unmoving, and so the world revolves. (3) 9.11–12: the deluded disregard him who has taken on a human body, not knowing his higher state as the great Lord of beings; of vain hopes, vain acts and vain knowledge, they resort to the deluding nature of rākṣasas and asuras. (4) 9.13–15: the great souls, resorting to the divine nature, worship him with minds on nothing else — always glorifying him, striving, firm in their vows, bowing to him with devotion, ever yoked — and others worship him by the sacrifice of knowledge, as one, as distinct and in many ways. (5) 9.16–19: he is the rite, the sacrifice, the offering to the ancestors, the healing plant, the mantra, the clarified butter, the fire and the oblation; the father, mother, sustainer and grandsire of the world, what is to be known, the purifier, Oṃ, and the Ṛk, Sāman and Yajus; the goal, upholder, lord, witness, dwelling, refuge and friend, the origin, dissolution, ground, treasure-house and imperishable seed; he gives heat, holds back and sends forth the rain; he is immortality and death, being and non-being. (6) 9.20–21: knowers of the three Vedas who have worshipped him with sacrifices pray for heaven, enjoy the world of the lord of the gods and, their merit spent, enter the world of mortals again — 'going and coming'. (7) 9.22–25: for those who, having no other, think of him and worship him, ever devoted, he himself carries acquisition and preservation; devotees of other deities who sacrifice with faith sacrifice to him, but not according to the rule; not recognising him in truth as the enjoyer and lord of all sacrifices, they fall; those vowed to the gods, the ancestors or the spirits go to them, and those who sacrifice to him come to him. (8) 9.26–28: a leaf, a flower, a fruit or water offered with devotion he partakes of; whatever Arjuna does, eats, offers, gives or practises as austerity is to be done as an offering to him, and so he will be freed from the bonds of action with their good and bad fruits and, yoked in the yoga of renunciation, will come to him. (9) 9.29–34: he is the same to all beings, none hateful or dear to him, but those who worship him with devotion are in him and he in them; even one of very bad conduct who worships him with undivided devotion is to be considered good and quickly becomes righteous — his devotee does not perish; those who take refuge in him, even those of sinful birth, women, vaiśyas and śūdras, go to the highest goal, how much more holy brāhmaṇas and devoted royal seers; closing injunction: having come to this impermanent, joyless world, worship him — mind on him, his devotee, sacrificing to him, bowing to him — and Arjuna will come to him. Purpose, as the chapter presents it: to declare the knowledge together with vijñāna (the phrase of 7.2) as the 'royal knowledge, royal secret' — the Lord who pervades all this and yet is not contained in it — and to present devotion to him, open to all who take refuge, as the way to him.
